=== notes:62
Conditional logic forms the bedrock of many arguments and inferences tested on the LSAT requiring test-takers to demonstrate proficiency in recognizing the contrapositive logic and avoiding common illegal moves. While the provided sources do not detail every single LSAT Logical Reasoning question type (such as Parallel Reasoning or Method of Reasoning) they offer a comprehensive breakdown of the core activities required for analysis and specifically target the most critical error tested in Flaw questions. The breakdown of conditional logic primarily revolves around two main analytical activities: Chaining (deriving new rules) and Triggering (deriving new facts). I. General Breakdown Activity: Chaining Conditionals (Deriving New Rules). Chaining involves synthesizing multiple conditional rules to create a new inferred conditional statement. This is crucial for questions that require you to draw broad conclusions from a set of premises (often seen in Inference/Must Be True contexts). Connecting Rules: You must determine if the necessary condition (right side) of one conditional statement matches the sufficient condition (left side) of another. For instance if A rightarrow B and B rightarrow C they chain to infer a new conditional relationship: A rightarrow C. Contraposing Chains: You can contraposit an entire chain starting from the final necessary condition and negating back to the initial sufficient condition. If the chain is A rightarrow D the contrapositive is If not D then not A. The LSAT commonly rewards the ability to connect the beginning of the chain to the ending of the chain. Negating Complex Ideas: If the idea being contraposed (the trigger or the outcome) is a complex idea connected by and or or (e.g. A and B) you must remember to flip the truth values when negating. And turns into or and or turns into and. This ensures you correctly flip the truth value of everything in the complex idea. II. General Breakdown Activity: Triggering Conditionals (Deriving New Facts). Triggering is the process of using a known fact to activate a conditional rule and derive a certain factual inference. This is the process utilized in many Must Be True and Inference questions. A conditional rule (Sufficient rightarrow Necessary) can only be used in two valid scenarios: 1. Affirming the Sufficient Condition: If a fact confirms that the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) is true then the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) must necessarily follow. 2. Denying the Necessary Condition (Contrapositive Logic): If a fact confirms that the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) is not true then the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) cannot have been true. If the fact relates to the conditional rule in any other way (e.g. affirming the necessary or denying the sufficient) the rule just disappears and has no power over us. III. Breakdown in Specific Question Types and Contexts. Flaw Questions. Flaw questions are specifically designed to test whether you can recognize errors in conditional reasoning. The Conditional Flaw: The specific mistake frequently made by LSAT authors is confusing the necessary and the sufficient. This happens when an argument presents a rule (A rightarrow B) observes that the right side (the necessary condition B) is happening and then attempts to conclude that this proves the left side (the sufficient condition A) is happening. Breakdown Method: Your breakdown must identify the conditional rule and then recognize that the author has committed the illegal move of reading the conditional backward. This is the primary error the LSAT tries to ensure students avoid. For example learning that Alice is in the USA (necessary condition) does not allow you to conclude she is in NYC (sufficient condition). Inference and Must Be True Questions. These questions rely heavily on the ability to correctly apply the chaining and triggering activities described above. Breakdown Method: When presented with premises (rules) and facts you must diagram the conditionals and perform valid inferences. You must look for ways to chain rules together to find a connection from the premises to the answer choices.

students\_other-sessions\2025-10-30-fiorella-notes-by-gemini.md:22: Joshua Fisseha's Presentation provided a detailed introduction to conditional reasoning, defining sufficient (the "if" part) and necessary (the "then" part) conditions, explaining that only the presence of the sufficient condition guarantees the necessary outcome, while the absence of the sufficient condition yields no conclusion. The presentation focused on identifying conditional indicator words, diagramming statements using arrows, and generating the contrapositive, which is the only other valid inference made by flipping and negating the conditions. Joshua Fisseha's Presentation and Fiorella Meyer worked through an example applying the concepts, reiterating that only the conditional statement and its contrapositive are known to be true, and the session concluded with homework emphasizing mechanical practice for proficiency, noting that the topic is fundamental to many LSAT question types.
students\_other-sessions\2025-12-18-sonja-notes-by-gemini.md:22: Joshua Fisseha's Presentation and About That Book discussed strategies for solving LSAT questions, focusing on prioritizing intuition over abstract diagramming for conditional statements and using meticulous reading to internalize information rather than rereading, which wastes time. Key techniques included anticipating conclusions, identifying striking structural features like "or" in parallel questions for efficient elimination, and focusing on the necessary condition as the focal point of conditional arguments. They also established a systematic, multi-step template for About That Book to formalize their approach, emphasizing the identification of structural cues, testing the bounds of claims (like the contrapositive), and addressing the categorical error that confuses subsets with the entire set in parallel flaw questions.

     1	
     2	PAGE 1
     3	LSAT Logical Reasoning: Contrapositives and
     4	Conditionals
     5	Overview of Conditional Statements
     6	Group 1: Sufficient Condition Indicators
     7	Keywords: IF, WHEN, WHERE, ALL, THE ONLY, EVERY, ANY
     8	Rule: Introduces the sufficient condition.
     9	Group 2: Necessary Condition Indicators
    10	Keywords: ONLY IF, ONLY WHEN, ONLY WHERE, ONLY, ALWAYS, MUST, REQUIRES
    11	Rule: Introduces the necessary condition.
    12	Steps to Analyze Conditional Statements
    13	1. Identify the logical operator.
    14	2. Identify the two main concepts (groups, categories, events, ideas).
    15	3. Assign symbols to the two main concepts.
    16	4. Apply the translation rule.
    17	5. Find the contrapositive.
    18	6. Translate back to English.
    19	Understanding Contrapositives
    20	Definition: Contrapositives are statements that are logically equivalent to the
    21	original conditional statement.
    22	Properties: They are literally the same relationship expressed in reverse form,
    23	the only thing logically equivalent to the original relationship.
    24	Example:
    25	English: All dogs are unselfish.
    26	Lawgic: D → ¬S
    27	Contrapositive in English: If something is selfish, then it’s not a dog.
    28	Contrapositive in Lawgic: S → ¬D
    29	Key Concept: Contrapositives represent the only other way in "Lawgic" to convey
    30	a specific conditional relationship.
    31	How to Formulate a Contrapositive(FLIP AND NEGATE )
    32	1. Switch the symbols around the arrow.
    33	2. Apply negation to each symbol.
    34	3. There is no Step 3; it's a simple two-step process to ensure clarity.
    35	Importance of Contrapositives in LSAT
    36	Application: Essential for solving logical reasoning problems effectively.
    37	Strategy: Often provides the correct perspective to answer tricky questions
    38	correctly.
    39	Highlights
    40	Critical Insight: "Contrapositives will save your life on the LSAT."
    41	
    42	PAGE 2
    43	Example of Logical Equivalence: The contrapositive S → ¬D is as valid as D →
    44	¬S, The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your
    45	conditional relationship. The LSAT will tempt you to erroneously assume other
    46	things from your conditional. Don't fall for these ploys. For example, they
    47	will give you a conditional statement and then say, "We know the necessary
    48	condition is happening!" When they do this, you should be like, "Cool. So I can
    49	ignore the conditional." The presence of the necessary condition doesn't make
    50	anything happen.
    51	Conditional Relationship Activation Chart
    52	Situation What has to happen?
    53	Sufficient
    54	Present I'm breathing, so there must be air.
    55	Necessary
    56	Absent There's no air, so I can't breathe.
    57	Sufficient
    58	Absent
    59	Nothing! There's no breathing happening, so we don't know
    60	anything else.
    61	Necessary
    62	Present Nothing! There's air around, so we don't know anything else.
    63	The last two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything
    64	happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes
    65	the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than that, two things can
    66	happen and you don't need to do anything with your conditional. Without the situations
    67	in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.

42: 1. **Keep the original rule visible.** If you are enrolled in law school, you are a student. Show complete conditions on either side of the original arrow.
43: 2. **Change one fact.** This person is not a student. Ask whether the person can be enrolled in law school. Require an attempt before revealing the explanation.
44: 3. **Explain why.** Enrollment requires being a student. Someone who is not a student cannot satisfy that requirement, so they cannot be enrolled under this rule.
45: 4. **Name the inference.** Proposed copy: “The contrapositive tells us what follows when the necessary condition is false.” Immediately read the concrete version: “If you are not a student, you cannot be enrolled in law school.” This is proposed wording, not a user-approved quotation.
46: 5. **Show both forms.** Original: enrolled → student. Contrapositive: not student → not enrolled. Keep both visible; present each new arrow left to right. It is the same relationship expressed another way, not an additional assumption.
47: 6. **Compare the four facts.** Enrolled establishes student; not student rules out enrolled; student alone does not establish enrollment; not enrolled alone does not settle student status. Use one clearly labeled fact at a time with a learner prediction.
48: 7. **Fresh check.** Use a clean existing requirement example with no modality or timing ambiguity. Check the inference, not recall of the label.
50: Design: large complete-condition labels, clear given-fact versus conclusion distinction, meaningful transition from the original necessary condition to its negation. Do not animate a bare reversal as though it were valid. Keep a static reduced-motion version. The color identity of a concept can remain consistent, but original sufficient/necessary labels must not falsely persist in the transformed rule: not-student is sufficient in the contrapositive and not-enrolled is necessary there.
52: Do not start with symbols or the phrase flip and negate. Do not claim a rule disappears when a fact leaves the other side undecided. Do not teach temporal or causal conclusions from the arrow.
54: ## Lesson 2: Write and read the contrapositive
56: Placement: after forward translation and the whole-meaning checks, before full worksheet contraposition. Reuse the existing formal lesson and its sign-pattern interactions.
58: 1. Read the original diagram in ordinary English.
59: 2. Reverse the positions of its two complete conditions.
60: 3. Negate both complete conditions. Explain not before introducing the symbol; negating not-A gives A.
61: 4. Read the new arrow back into English and compare its meaning with the original.
62: 5. Work through A → B; A → not-B; not-A → B; not-A → not-B. Derive each transformation rather than displaying four unexplained formulas.
63: 6. Distinguish a contrapositive from reversal alone and negation alone using short correction tasks.
64: 7. Explain why these training drills ask students to write the contrapositive, while later test-taking need not involve writing it every time.
66: Whole-condition negation comes before compound decomposition. Not returned unopened does not automatically mean returned opened; not both is not neither. Advanced AND/OR and quantified scope items stay outside this lesson until their own prerequisites are taught.
68: ## Where every prototype drill goes
70: | Prototype set | Items | Core-path placement | Adaptation |
71: |---|---:|---|---|
72: | D1 Intro identification | 15 | After students have learned both indicator groups and condition roles | Select indicator(s), then full sufficient and necessary conditions. Preserve scope/modality. Intro training is the first source-backed drill. |
73: | D2 Indicator identification | 13 | Follow D1 as focused word-family practice, or use targeted subsets following each group's instruction | Label the selected expression, not the entire sentence. Correct the-only conflict. Avoid falsely forcing one group when more than one indicator is present. |
74: | D4 Indicator roles | 10 | After complete-condition teaching, before independent rewriting | Reuse familiar statements to place both conditions beside an arrow. Explicitly identify this as a new operation on familiar examples. |
75: | D3 Intro diagrams | 15 | After role placement; forward-only first, revisit after formal contraposition | Provide distinct original-only and original-plus-contrapositive task variants. Remove premature optional contrapositive controls from the early version. |
76: | D5 If/then rephrasing | 25 | Keep the whole meaning / mixed forward translation | Write a faithful if/then version and diagram it. Later revisit selected items with contraposition. Split into short sessions, keeping all available in the bank. |
77: | D6 Full indicator translation | 20 | After formal contrapositive lesson and readiness check | Original diagram, contrapositive, and English readbacks. Separate simple from negative/temporal/compound items. |
78: | D7 Independent translation | 35 | Final mixed practice and assignable review | Fresh mixed tasks after prerequisites. Hold flawed/unclear sources and compound-scope items until corrected/taught. Do not force a single 35-item sitting. |
80: Thus the likely working order is D1 → D2 → D4 → D3 original-only → D5 forward-only → formal contraposition → D3/D5 revisit → D6 → D7. This is a learning sequence, not a requirement to complete every available item before advancing. Final numbering must reflect the actual chosen path rather than the order in which source files were prototyped.
82: ## How these fit the material already built
84: - Keep the definition and Must arrow lessons. Move and clarify missing-requirement discovery, rather than making a third contrapositive destination.
85: - Keep brief checks next to the idea they assess. Audit the existing 24-question Drill 1 against new D1/D2: retain distinct conceptual questions as lesson checks or supplementary practice; remove duplicate mandatory work only after item-level comparison. Do not silently delete that bank.
86: - Use the new source items to deepen complete-condition and forward-translation practice. The existing mixed engine already supports attempts, diagramming, equivalence checks and error-specific follow-up, so reuse those mechanics where they fit.
87: - Keep the nine-item final integrated drill as a short readiness check. D6/D7 supply sustained practice after it.
88: - Keep flashcards separate for recall. They do not substitute for writing or constructing an equivalent rule.
89: - Keep all source sets accessible from a drill library, even when the recommended path assigns a shorter subset.
91: ## Implementation plan
93: ### Pass 1: Clean and structure the source bank

Record file/hash/page/item, original statement, reviewed derivative, source issues, complete conditions, indicator spans, forward relation, optional contrapositive, accepted paraphrases, explanations and prerequisite tags. Do not count typing a draft as mastery.

Open prose can receive a model explanation and an explicit self-check or teacher-review state; do not present it as automatically validated if no reliable grading exists.
111: ### Pass 5: Add full contraposition practice

115: ### Pass 6: Verify real student use

For recorded/assignable work, use the app's existing attempt and persistence contracts, then verify reload, resume, completion and assignment behavior. The standalone prototype's browser-local writing fields are not production assessment or cross-device persistence.

First concrete implementation should be the conceptual contrapositive lesson plus cleaned Intro Training, reviewed together before bulk importing the later sets.

234 The Contrapositive
236 So far, we’ve explored the sufficient condition, the necessary condition, and the relationship between the two. Specifically, we’ve gone over the core of the conditional relationship:
238 If the sufficient condition occurs, then the necessary condition must occur.
240 This relationship is ironclad. We’ll call it the Must Relationship. Remember this as we explore a conditional thought experiment.
242 Imagine you’re a coffee-obsessed individual. If you’re walking, you’re on your way to get a coffee. Getting a coffee is the only reason you ever walk anywhere. Every single time you walk, you are getting a coffee.
244 Now, imagine that you are not on your way to get a coffee.
246 What do you know about yourself?
248 Put your guess here: ______________________________
250 STOP
252 Really try to figure this out right now. Don’t read the solution until you have a guess. I put this question to every student. The ones that hazard a guess (either voluntarily or under protest) always come away better off.
254 If you are not on your way to get a coffee, you haven’t met your necessary burden, right? So could you have activated your sufficient condition? NO.
256 The fact that you are not on your way to get a coffee means you are not walking.
258 REMEMBER!
259 If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive.
261 As you’ve seen, the contrapositive can be produced organically. It is not some foreign process that needs to be performed on conditionals. The contrapositive is sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized. If you have not met your necessary burden, you cannot have activated the sufficient condition.
263 Let’s return to our original example to see how its contrapositive looks:
265 If I’m breathing, then air is present.
267 OK, so if I’m breathing, air is needed. I have to have air for the breathing to happen. The air is completely necessary. What if I don’t have air? That means I don’t have the thing I need. That implies that I couldn’t have activated my conditional relationship. So, I can’t be breathing. No necessary conditions means there’s no sufficient condition.
269 The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your conditional relationship. The LSAT will try to tempt you into erroneously surmising other things from your conditional. Don’t fall for these ploys. For example, they will give you a conditional statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.
271 Check out this chart showing what does and does not activate your conditional relationship:
275 SituationsWhat Has to Happen?If I’m Breathing, Then Air Is Present.
278 Sufficient PresentMust have necessary conditionI’m breathing, so there must be air.
279 Necessary AbsentCan’t have sufficient conditionThere’s no air, so I can’t breathe.
280 Sufficient AbsentNothing!There’s no breathing happening, so we don’t know anything else.
281 Necessary PresentNothing!There’s air around, so we don’t know anything else.
285 The first two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than those two things can happen and you don’t need to do anything with your conditional. Without the situations in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.
289 How to Diagram Conditionals
291 Now that we have a handle on the Must Relationship, let’s take a look at how to represent it symbolically.
350 I see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a B in the sufficient condition:
352 B →
354 Now, I’ll fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with A for air, since that’s what’s left in the statement.
356 B → A
358 There you go. Your first fully diagrammed conditional! Now let’s talk through the contrapositive. If the necessary condition is gone, we know the sufficient is gone as well. We can diagram the contrapositive as:
360 ~A → ~B
362 Check out the little squiggle (~) in front of the ~A and ~B. We’ll use the squiggle throughout this text to negate variables. Read the squiggle as a “not.”
364 Check out a few more contrapositive examples:
367 Original StatementEquivalent Contrapositive
369 A → B~B → ~A
370 Q → ~RR → ~Q
371 ~L → P~P → L
372 ~Y → ~XX → Y
376 Once you have the original statement written down, you can flip and negate both sides to quickly jot down the contrapositive.
378 I don’t recommend diagramming the contrapositive on every conditional you encounter just for the sake of doing so, but sometimes it will be useful to write the conditional in its contrapositive form, either for clarity or to facilitate a chain. Conditionals can be written either way: as the original statement or as the contrapositive. Both statements are describing the same relationship.
381 Instead of writing the contrapositive out every time, you can just read the contrapositive when it’s relevant. Every conditional can be read two ways:
383 Read it forwards from left to right.
384 Read it backwards from right to left, negating everything as you go (reading the contrapositive without having to write it out).
388 Awesome! Let’s talk through some frequent diagramming issues together.
390 Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ
393 Q.When should I diagram a conditional?
394 A.
395 In the beginning, you should always diagram conditionals. This trains you to make sure you’re actually thinking conditionally. The language in LR is your biggest obstacle. Diagramming the conditional gets rid of all the linguistic ambiguity. You don’t have to look back at the text once you’re sure you diagrammed the conditional correctly. (I’ll get to how you’ll be sure in a second. Hold tight.)
396 When you are more experienced, you should diagram whenever the stimulus is not easy. I always err on the side of diagramming; I think of it as using the paper to my advantage. If I can take down a note to free myself of the exact words of the stimulus, I’m going to do it. I see far more problems from students declining to diagram than I do from students who diagram too much. If there is more than one conditional in a stimulus, you should diagram them to see if there is chaining potential.
397 Besides timing concerns, I mostly see people decline to diagram because they aren’t confident that their diagram will be correct, and they feel safer sticking to the exact words of the stimulus because then they “can’t screw it up.” But you need to fail a couple times in order to build the skill. That’s how you get better. Don’t give up before you start. This leads me to the next question.
400 Q.How will I ever be sure my conditional is correctly diagrammed?
401 A.
402 Seeing it work out correctly enough times breeds confidence. In your Wrong Answer Journal, keep track of every time you incorrectly diagram a conditional and make note of exactly how the language tricked you. Study these errors so you don’t make the same mistake again. Your mistakes are your greatest asset.
403 The only way to ever be 100% sure that your diagram is right is confidence in your own abilities. There will never be a little green checkmark that spontaneously appears on the page to tell you that you’re right.
404 Everyone messes this up in the beginning. You can’t let that kill your confidence. Do the work, build skill, and you will eventually know that you’re right.
407 Q.But diagramming takes sooooo much time. I need to be fast. Do I really have to?
408 A.
409 Diagramming does not take nearly as much time as you imagine it does. There is a huge gap between perception and reality when it comes to LR timing. It takes a lot less time to diagram than it does to reread a difficult sentence ad infinitum. Writing things down frees up mental resources for other tasks. You need all the mental power you can get when you’re doing LR, so use the paper to your advantage.
410 Also, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.
413 Q.Should I always diagram the contrapositive?
414 A.
415 No. Many people claim that always diagramming the contrapositive saves time, but it actually ends up costing you much more time than it saves, especially on Logic Games. You should understand conditional reasoning on a deep enough level to look at the original conditional diagram and know that the contrapositive is also true (reading the contrapositive instead of writing it). This works for the same reason you knew the contrapositive was true in our initial contrapositive thought experiment. The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement. If you can write it, you can already read it, so don’t waste time putting extra noise on the paper.
416 That said, there are times when diagramming the contrapositive is useful, and you should definitely diagram it if you have a reason to do so. Sometimes you’ll need the contrapositive to facilitate a chain. Just don’t write the contrapositive out of habit and you’ll do great.
420 You’ll diagram the contrapositive in all the drills in this chapter. Why? I want to verify that you have the ability to identify the contrapositive. Once you see that your contrapositives match the drill answer keys, you’ll be more confident simply reading the contrapositive in your head from the original statement.

# Sufficient Assumption: Concept Router & File Catalog

This subfolder houses all lecture transcriptions, teleprompter scripts, visual media, and analytical teardowns for **Sufficient Assumption (SA)** questions within the Loophole Lecture curriculum.

---

## What is Sufficient Assumption?

In Logical Reasoning, a Sufficient Assumption question stem asks you to supply an unstated assumption that, when added to the argument premises, forces the conclusion to be one hundred percent logically valid. 

Unlike Necessary Assumption questions (which demand minimalist, weak assumptions), Sufficient Assumption questions demand powerful, binding rules (either a bridge between mismatched terms or an existential trigger activating a contrapositive chain).

### Core Mathematical Archetypes

1. **The Chain Bridge (Form A):**
   - Premise 1: $A \rightarrow B$
   - Premise 2: $B \rightarrow C$
   - Conclusion: $A \rightarrow D$
   - **Missing Sufficient Assumption:** $C \rightarrow D$ (or contrapositive: $\sim D \rightarrow \sim C$).

2. **The Existential Trigger (Form B):**
   - Premise Chain: $A \rightarrow B \rightarrow C \rightarrow D$
   - Conclusion: $\sim A$ (factual denial)
   - **Missing Sufficient Assumption:** A factual claim establishing $\sim D$ (which cascades through the contrapositives to prove $\sim A$).

---

125: ### Section 6: Anticipating the Contrapositive on Test Day
126: **Timestamp: [06:15 - 07:15]**
128: > **[06:15] Ellen Cassidy:** "Here is the catch, and this is why students get tripped up on Section 4:  
129: > The test writers know that you are looking for `Awkward -> Unpleasant`. They know that is the intuitive forward direction."  
130: > 
131: > **[06:35] Ellen Cassidy:** "So what do they do in the answer choices? They hide it in the contrapositive!  
132: > What is the contrapositive of `Awkward -> Unpleasant`?  
133: > Flip and negate both terms:  
134: > `~Unpleasant Drinking Experience -> ~Awkward Cold Beverages`."  
135: > 
136: > **[06:55] Ellen Cassidy:** "In plain English, that sounds like:  
137: > *Any drinking experience that is not unpleasant cannot involve awkward cold beverages.*  
138: > It sounds clunky and backward when you read it in the choices, but logically it is identical to our bridge equation. If you do not actively prephrase the contrapositive, you might stare right at the correct answer and miss it."

17: # Conditional Must Be True: Keep the Whole Statement
19: Only people willing to compromise should undergo mediation. This tells you what is required for mediation to be appropriate. It does not tell you who actually attends, or whether willingness alone is enough. Keeping those distinctions intact is the work of conditional reasoning.
21: ## Combine what the statements guarantee
23: A Must Be True answer must follow from the information you were given. Sometimes you combine facts. Sometimes you connect rules. Identify what each sentence gives you before choosing a method.
25: Suppose one type of flavonoid found in apples is an antioxidant, and antioxidants help prevent heart disease. You can conclude that at least one type of flavonoid found in apples helps prevent heart disease.
27: Why at least one? The first statement identifies one type with the relevant property. The second tells you what that property does. Together they support a claim about that type. They do not show that every flavonoid has the property, or that eating any apple prevents heart disease.
29: Keep the strength of the conclusion equal to the strength of the evidence. Helps prevent does not mean always prevents.
31: A rule works differently. If a person should undergo mediation, that person is willing to compromise tells you a relationship. It does not tell you that anyone actually should undergo mediation. A rule does not establish its own starting condition.
33: ### Check your reasoning
35: An apple flavonoid is an antioxidant, and antioxidants help prevent heart disease. Can you conclude that every apple flavonoid prevents heart disease? Explain.
37: **Worked answer:** No. The evidence establishes the relevant property for at least one type, not every type. It says helps prevent, not always prevents. A second type without that property would fit the evidence and defeat the stronger answer.
39: ## Put the requirement on the right

6. "Every conditional has a partner. They're like interchangeable. Everything else you do not know." `students/sandra/sessions/2026-09-28-conditional-logic-foundations/transcript.md:100`
7. "There's only one logically equivalent statement. One statement that's equally true." Same file, line 246. Also line 260: "Statement one, statement two. They're logically equivalent... I can't know anything else for sure."
8. "Contrapositive just means the logically equivalent statement. It's the only other statement." When Charles said it is "a different way of writing the original equation," he answered: "Yeah." `students/charles-moore/transcript-downloads/2026-09-09-charles-moore-notes-by-gemini.md:395,409-413`
9. "The contrapositive is the logically equivalent statement. So they're identical statements." `students/zeina-borji/sessions/2026-09-25-conditional-foundations/transcript.md:936` (and line 944: "they can be used interchangeably").
10. "Smart" students suffer here: "you could almost get dumber... I only know one other thing to be true outside what is being stated." `students/ivory-johnson/sessions/2026-09-19-session/transcript.md:179`
11. "The contrapositive isn't intuitive." Same file, line 207; line 237: "we're actually trained to not think in a strict logical way."
12. "We don't read right to left. We only read left to right... Just because I meet someone at the train station I can't assume they took the same transportation." `NFWI/Moses - 2025_11_03 18_00 EST - Notes by Gemini.md:431`
13. "The contrapositive is equally valid. If I say if not conclusion then not premise, that's equally valid too." `students/vinay-kalva/sessions/2026-08-29-vinay-lsat-session/transcript.md:567` (and 495).
14. "If you didn't understand that the contrapositive is equally valid, that would have made this question way harder." `students/ivory-johnson/sessions/2026-10-03-sufficient-assumption-pathways/transcript.md:285`

## 2. Handles, phrasings and mental moves

- **Vocabulary for the equivalence.** He uses "logically equivalent," "evil twin," "dance partners," "interchangeable," "equally valid," "synonymous" (Sandra:252) and "a different way of saying the original" (Charles). The usual line: "Every conditional has exactly one partner."
- **Two ways to get there.**
  - The mechanical route is "flip and negate," in two steps, also called "swap and slash" (Dakota:507-516, Fiorella:255). Put the slash through both ideas.
  - The meaning route works backward from the necessary condition: if the requirement fails, the guarantee never fired. Examples are Iowa/US, NYC/US, breathing/air, the Eden fruit/death rule (Sandra:88-102) and arson (Ivory:195-227).
- **Activation framing.** "You only know what happens when these two triggers get pulled" (Asia:284). The left side fires the rule, and the contrapositive fires when the right side is denied. "Anything else I say, you don't know" (Moses:501-508).
- **Brain-off discipline.** "The dumber you are, the easier" (Thaniuska 2026-03-09:646). "Turn your brain off" (Ivory 09-19:269). Intuition is the enemy. Dakota (05-02:468): "when you try to be creative is when you get punished."
- **Say it in plain English, not symbols.** "Say what the contrapositive would mean in normal English... turning it too much into conditional machine-wise can get in the way" (`NFWI/Sonja - 2025 10 31 18_43 EDT - Notes by Gemini.md:978-980`). He says to use a "personalized if-then."
- **Diagram to train, not to perform.** "We're diagramming so that we don't have to diagram" (Ivory 09-19:207). Also: "don't force yourself to diagram... it's better to just say the contrapositive" (`NFWI/Meeting started 2025_10_30 20_04 EDT:127-129`).
- **Negation is not opposition.** Rich becomes "not rich," not "poor," and hot becomes "not hot," not "cold." Negation is "adding a not" (Dakota 05-02:525-537, Dillen:273-281). A double negative turns positive (Sandra:287-289).
- **Chains.** The contrapositive of a chain: "negate from the point you're asked about, and negate everything prior to it. Nothing after it" (Zeina 10-02:1153-1163, Moses:559, Asia:319-327).

## 3. Mistakes he warns about

- **Reversal or "read backwards."** Dakota:458 and Moses:431. Also the sufficient/necessary mix-up: Ivory 09-19:227, "I didn't say the only way to be guilty of arson is burning a building down."
- **Denying the sufficient or affirming the necessary.** "Not Kenyan" yields nothing, and neither does "is African" (Asia:280-284). Sofia, 09-11:1015-1017: "you can't negate the sufficient."
- **Half-flips.** Amanda negated without swapping, or swapped without negating (09-22:637, 659, 649). Ivory 09-19 teaching-notes: "reversing or negating only one side does not follow."
- **Stating the "false contrapositive" as bait.** Asia:265 and Dillen:309-317. The true contrapositive often sits in the answer choices looking odd: "they're going to try to bait you with a negation of the law."
- **Compounds.** For a requirement list, "it just requires one of those to be missing" (Azaria 10-26:619; Sabrina, `corpus/Curriculum/Necessary Assumption/Notes/_scan_Sabrina...EST - Notes by Gemini.md:856`; NFWI Meeting 2025_12_22:1503). He says "and" turns to "or" (the megadoc `_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:62` states the rule, but its authorship is unverified).
- **Negating "more."** "Not more" means "at least as" (Kevin 2025-11-29:709-731).
- **Group 3 and 4 words.** "Never," "cannot," "not both" go to the right side, negated. With "cannot" plus "unless," apply the unless rule once (Zeina teaching-notes).
- **Which terms to use.** Charles asked about converse and inverse. His answer: "no no just contrapositive" (09-09 downloads:398-399).

## 4. Link to question types

- **Must be true / inference.** This is the heaviest use: "they're going to test your ability to see the contrapositive applied to the situation." He says it is "almost always" the contrapositive (Sofia 09-11:649-651,679; Azaria:694; Moses:508, "maybe half the questions"; Charles 09-14:351-367). "A fact can trigger the rule or the contrapositive." Contrapositives apply only to rules, not facts (Charles 09-14:353).
- **Chains.** The answer is "head to tail" (Charles:395-412, Sofia:635, Asia:308). If you panic: "oh, look for the contrapositive" (Charles:399-401). "They might throw the contrapositive as a plot twist" (Sofia 09-23:451).
- **Sufficient assumption.** A hidden path requires contraposing a link (Ivory 10-03:438-450, 285-287). The bridge may appear in contrapositive form (Vinay:567-587; Sofia 09-25:261). His SA notes say "the correct answer loves to put that connection in contrapositive form" (`corpus/Curriculum/Sufficient Assumption/Notes/_megadoc_notes_sufficient_assumption.md:63`, from "Sufficient Assumption.gdoc"). I could not verify that this gdoc is his own writing.
- **Necessary assumption.** The tie is thin. Sabrina (Necessary Assumption note, line 191) has him say "the rule they give you, the contrapositive equally applies," so the facts apply to either form. Moses:508 links it to "what is missing from the argument."
- **Flaw.** He calls it a "false reversal" or "false contrapositive" (Gianna 10-08:657; Mounosha 2026-01-25:135-156).

## 5. Beyond or against the framing

- **The framing is confirmed.** "If the left is true, the right is true; if the right is false, the left is false" matches his usage almost word for word (Fiorella:192; Thaniuska 03-09:648).
- **Beyond the framing:**
  - He adds a closed-world claim: "there is only one other thing you know." Everything else is unknown, not false.
  - He adds the activation framing, the "personalized/plain English readback" move, and the "contrapositive as bait in answer choices" point.
  - He adds a practice-versus-test split. Drill with diagrams, then read it in your head.
  - He adds that you should "do the contrapositive up front... it will become subconscious" (NFWI Meeting 2025_12_22:1430).
- **Looseness or conflicts to be aware of:**
  - "Everything else you don't know by default is wrong" (Fiorella:264) and "Everything else is false" (Charles 09-09:413). Elsewhere he says "unknown." These should not be quoted as "false."
  - "Only one logically equivalent statement" is a teaching simplification. Other equivalent rewrites exist, such as "not A or B."
  - He once calls the contrapositive "the inverse" (Sabrina:762, Meghan 03-17:171), which clashes with standard terminology.
  - "A rule disappears when a fact leaves the other side undecided" is the voice-standard's phrasing, and the project plan says not to claim it.
- **Third-party material, not Joshua's words:**
  - The Joshua-selected "voice standard" HTML (`raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html:254-261, 378-420`) has a coffee/walking thought experiment and the line "the contrapositive is contained within the original statement." Its authorship is unidentified, and it mentions Logic Games.
  - "Conditional intro" handout (`brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/source-audit-2026-10-11/notes/2025-09-03_Conditional intro.txt:19-50`): "literally the same relationship expressed in reverse form." Authorship unverified.
  - Lecture 33013 and the Loophole SA transcript are other presenters.
- **Proposed wording that is not Joshua's** (`brainstorms/.../2026-10-11-contrapositive-and-drill-integration-plan.md:45-46`): "It is the same relationship expressed another way, not an additional assumption." The file itself marks this as "proposed wording, not a user-approved quotation."
